    /// Checks whether `addr..addr + size` is covered by a single `VmMapping`,
    /// and returns the address of the single `VmMapping` if successful.
    fn check_lies_in_single_mapping(&self, addr: Vaddr, size: usize) -> Result<Vaddr> {
        if let Some(vm_mapping) = self
            .vm_mappings
            .find_one(&addr)
            .filter(|vm_mapping| vm_mapping.map_end() - addr >= size)
        {
            Ok(vm_mapping.map_to_addr())
        } else {
            return_errno_with_message!(Errno::EFAULT, "the range must lie in a single mapping");
        }
    }

    /// Inserts a `VmMapping` into the `Vmar`, without attempting to merge with
    /// neighboring mappings.
    ///
    /// The caller must ensure that the given `VmMapping` is not mergeable with
    /// any neighboring mappings.
    ///
    /// Make sure the insertion doesn't exceed address space limit.
    fn insert_without_try_merge(&mut self, vm_mapping: VmMapping) {
        self.total_vm += vm_mapping.map_size();
        self.vm_mappings.insert(vm_mapping);
    }

    /// Inserts a `VmMapping` into the `Vmar`, and attempts to merge it with
    /// neighboring mappings.
    ///
    /// This method will try to merge the `VmMapping` with neighboring mappings
    /// that are adjacent and compatible, in order to reduce fragmentation.
    ///
    /// Make sure the insertion doesn't exceed address space limit.
    fn insert_try_merge(&mut self, vm_mapping: VmMapping) {
        self.total_vm += vm_mapping.map_size();
        let mut vm_mapping = vm_mapping;
        let addr = vm_mapping.map_to_addr();

        if let Some(prev) = self.vm_mappings.find_prev(&addr) {
            let (new_mapping, to_remove) = vm_mapping.try_merge_with(prev);
            vm_mapping = new_mapping;
            if let Some(addr) = to_remove {
                self.vm_mappings.remove(&addr);
            }
        }

        if let Some(next) = self.vm_mappings.find_next(&addr) {
            let (new_mapping, to_remove) = vm_mapping.try_merge_with(next);
            vm_mapping = new_mapping;
            if let Some(addr) = to_remove {
                self.vm_mappings.remove(&addr);
            }
        }

        self.vm_mappings.insert(vm_mapping);
    }

    /// Removes a `VmMapping` based on the provided key from the `Vmar`.
    fn remove(&mut self, key: &Vaddr) -> Option<VmMapping> {
        let vm_mapping = self.vm_mappings.remove(key)?;
        self.total_vm -= vm_mapping.map_size();
        Some(vm_mapping)
    }

    /// Finds a set of [`VmMapping`]s that intersect with the provided range.
    fn query(&self, range: &Range<Vaddr>) -> impl Iterator<Item = &VmMapping> {
        self.vm_mappings.find(range)
    }

    /// Calculates the total amount of overlap between `VmMapping`s
    /// and the provided range.
    fn count_overlap_size(&self, range: Range<Vaddr>) -> usize {
        let mut sum_overlap_size = 0;
        for vm_mapping in self.vm_mappings.find(&range) {
            let vm_mapping_range = vm_mapping.range();
            let intersected_range = get_intersected_range(&range, &vm_mapping_range);
            sum_overlap_size += intersected_range.end - intersected_range.start;
        }
        sum_overlap_size
    }

    /// Allocates a free region for mapping with a specific offset and size.
    ///
    /// If the provided range is already occupied, return an error.
    fn alloc_free_region_exact(&mut self, offset: Vaddr, size: usize) -> Result<Range<Vaddr>> {
        if self
            .vm_mappings
            .find(&(offset..offset + size))
            .next()
            .is_some()
        {
            return_errno_with_message!(Errno::EACCES, "Requested region is already occupied");
        }

        Ok(offset..(offset + size))
    }

    /// Allocates a free region for mapping with a specific offset and size.
    ///
    /// If the provided range is already occupied, this function truncates all
    /// the mappings that intersect with the range.
    fn alloc_free_region_exact_truncate(
        &mut self,
        vm_space: &VmSpace,
        offset: Vaddr,
        size: usize,
        rss_delta: &mut RssDelta,
    ) -> Result<Range<Vaddr>> {
        let range = offset..offset + size;
        let mut mappings_to_remove = Vec::new();
        for vm_mapping in self.vm_mappings.find(&range) {
            mappings_to_remove.push(vm_mapping.map_to_addr());
        }

        for vm_mapping_addr in mappings_to_remove {
            let vm_mapping = self.remove(&vm_mapping_addr).unwrap();
            let vm_mapping_range = vm_mapping.range();
            let intersected_range = get_intersected_range(&range, &vm_mapping_range);

            let (left, taken, right) = vm_mapping.split_range(&intersected_range);
            if let Some(left) = left {
                self.insert_without_try_merge(left);
            }
            if let Some(right) = right {
                self.insert_without_try_merge(right);
            }

            rss_delta.add(taken.rss_type(), -(taken.unmap(vm_space) as isize));
        }

    /// Allocates a free region for mapping.
    ///
    /// If no such region is found, return an error.
    fn alloc_free_region(&mut self, size: usize, align: usize) -> Result<Range<Vaddr>> {
        // Fast path that there's still room to the end.
        let highest_occupied = self
            .vm_mappings
            .iter()
            .next_back()
            .map_or(ROOT_VMAR_LOWEST_ADDR, |vm_mapping| vm_mapping.range().end);
        // FIXME: The up-align may overflow.
        let last_occupied_aligned = highest_occupied.align_up(align);
        if let Some(last) = last_occupied_aligned.checked_add(size) {
            if last <= ROOT_VMAR_CAP_ADDR {
                return Ok(last_occupied_aligned..last);
            }
        }

        // Slow path that we need to search for a free region.
        // Here, we use a simple brute-force FIRST-FIT algorithm.
        // Allocate as low as possible to reduce fragmentation.
        let mut last_end: Vaddr = ROOT_VMAR_LOWEST_ADDR;
        for vm_mapping in self.vm_mappings.iter() {
            let range = vm_mapping.range();

            debug_assert!(range.start >= last_end);
            debug_assert!(range.end <= highest_occupied);

            let last_aligned = last_end.align_up(align);
            let needed_end = last_aligned
                .checked_add(size)
                .ok_or(Error::new(Errno::ENOMEM))?;

            if needed_end <= range.start {
                return Ok(last_aligned..needed_end);
            }

    /// Represents a set of Linux capabilities.
    pub struct CapSet: u64 {

        const DAC_READ_SEARCH = 1 << 2;
        const FOWNER = 1 << 3;
        const FSETID = 1 << 4;
        const KILL = 1 << 5;
        const SETGID = 1 << 6;
        const SETUID = 1 << 7;
        const SETPCAP = 1 << 8;
        const LINUX_IMMUTABLE = 1 << 9;
        const NET_BIND_SERVICE = 1 << 10;
        const NET_BROADCAST = 1 << 11;
        const NET_ADMIN = 1 << 12;
        const NET_RAW = 1 << 13;

        const IPC_OWNER = 1 << 15;

        const SYS_RAWIO = 1 << 17;
        const SYS_CHROOT = 1 << 18;
        const SYS_PTRACE = 1 << 19;

        const SYS_ADMIN = 1 << 21;
        const SYS_BOOT = 1 << 22;
        const SYS_NICE = 1 << 23;
        const SYS_RESOURCE = 1 << 24;
        const SYS_TIME = 1 << 25;
        const SYS_TTY_CONFIG = 1 << 26;
        const MKNOD = 1 << 27;
        const LEASE = 1 << 28;
        const AUDIT_WRITE = 1 << 29;
        const AUDIT_CONTROL = 1 << 30;
        const SETFCAP = 1 << 31;
        const MAC_OVERRIDE = 1 << 32;
        const MAC_ADMIN = 1 << 33;
        const SYSLOG = 1 << 34;
        const WAKE_ALARM = 1 << 35;
        const BLOCK_SUSPEND = 1 << 36;
        const AUDIT_READ = 1 << 37;
        const PERFMON = 1 << 38;
        const BPF = 1 << 39;
        const CHECKPOINT_RESTORE = 1u64 << 40;
        // ... include other capabilities as needed
    }
}

impl CapSet {
    const MASK: u64 = (1 << (CapSet::most_significant_bit() + 1)) - 1;

    /// Converts the capability set to a `u32`. The higher bits are truncated.
    pub fn as_u32(&self) -> u32 {
        self.bits() as u32
    }

    /// Creates a new `CapSet` with a full capability set, typically for a root user.
    pub const fn new_root() -> Self {
        CapSet::all()
    }

    /// The most significant bit in a 64-bit `CapSet` that may be set to represent a Linux capability.
    pub const fn most_significant_bit() -> u8 {
        // CHECKPOINT_RESTORE is the Linux capability with the largest numerical value
        40
    }
}

impl TryFrom<u64> for CapSet {
    type Error = &'static str;

    fn try_from(value: u64) -> Result<Self, Self::Error> {
        if value & !CapSet::MASK != 0 {
            Err("Invalid CapSet.")
        } else {
            Ok(CapSet { bits: value })
        }
    }

## Maintainability

### `kernel/src/syscall/capget.rs` line 28

> ```diff
>     // Capget only query current process's credential. Namely, it only allows header->pid == 0
>     // or header->pid == getpid(), which are equivalent.
>     // See https://linux.die.net/man/2/capget (Section. With VFS capability support) for details.
> ```

`cite-sources` (minor): The `capget` comment cites `linux.die.net` and the `With VFS capability support` section, but the authoritative `capget(2)` page at <https://man7.org/linux/man-pages/man2/capget.2.html> uses that section for `capset()` restrictions; this sends readers to the wrong contract for the local `pid` limitation.

**Fix.** Cite the authoritative `man7.org` page and phrase the comment as an Asterinas implementation limitation, or update the comment to match the actual `capget(2)` contract.

### `kernel/src/syscall/madvise.rs` line 24

> ```diff
>     if len > isize::MAX as usize {
>         return_errno_with_message!(Errno::EINVAL, "len align overflow");
>     }
> ```

`error-message-format` (nit): The error message `len align overflow` is terse and grammatically unclear; it does not tell the next reader or caller which condition made `len` invalid.

**Fix.** Use a specific Linux-style message such as `` `len` is too large to align to a page boundary ``.

### `kernel/src/syscall/madvise.rs` line 39

**Fix.** Remove the comment if the code is self-explanatory, or replace it with the design reason for this approximation, for example that these advice modes are handled by prefaulting readable pages.

## Correctness

### `kernel/src/syscall/capget.rs` line 20

> ```diff
>     17    let cap_user_header: cap_user_header_t =
>     18        user_space.read_val::<cap_user_header_t>(cap_user_header_addr)?;
>     19
>     20    if cap_user_header.version != LINUX_CAPABILITY_VERSION_3 {
>     21        return_errno_with_message!(Errno::EINVAL, "not supported (capability version is not 3)");
>     22    };
> ```

Incorrect syscall result (major): When `hdrp->version` is unsupported, Linux `capget()` must fail with `EINVAL` and write the kernel's preferred capability version back to `hdrp->version`; this returns `EINVAL` without updating the user header, so version-probing callers cannot discover `LINUX_CAPABILITY_VERSION_3`: <https://man7.org/linux/man-pages/man2/capget.2.html>.

**Fix.** Make `cap_user_header` mutable, set `cap_user_header.version = LINUX_CAPABILITY_VERSION_3`, write it back to `cap_user_header_addr`, and then return `EINVAL` on unsupported versions.

### `kernel/src/syscall/capget.rs` line 29

> ```diff
>     24    // Extract target pid and validate whether it represents the current process.
>     25    let header_pid = cap_user_header.pid;
>     26    // Capget only query current process's credential. Namely, it only allows header->pid == 0
>     27    // or header->pid == getpid(), which are equivalent.
>     28    // See https://linux.die.net/man/2/capget (Section. With VFS capability support) for details.
>     29    if header_pid != 0 && header_pid != ctx.process.pid() {
> ```

Incorrect syscall result (major): `capget()` rejects any `hdrp->pid` other than `0` or the current process, but Linux allows `capget()` to probe another process/thread by `pid`; the VFS-only-current restriction applies to `capset()`, not `capget()`: <https://man7.org/linux/man-pages/man2/capget.2.html>.

**Fix.** For `capget()`, look up the target thread/process when `header_pid != 0`, return `ESRCH` if it does not exist, and report that target's credentials. Keep the current-process restriction only in `capset()`.

### `kernel/src/syscall/capget.rs` line 43

> ```diff
>     40    let result = cap_user_data_t {
>     41        // Note we silently drop the upper capabilities here.
>     42        // This behavior is considered fail-safe behavior.
>     43        effective: effective_capset.as_u32(),
>     44        permitted: permitted_capset.as_u32(),
>     45        inheritable: inheritable_capset.as_u32(),
>     46    };
>     47
>     48    user_space.write_val(cap_user_data_addr, &result)?;
> ```

Incorrect syscall result (major): `LINUX_CAPABILITY_VERSION_3` uses two `cap_user_data_t` entries, but this writes only one and truncates every capability above bit `31`. A process with `CAP_BPF` or `CAP_CHECKPOINT_RESTORE` set will get `0` in the upper capability word instead of the real value. Linux documents `_LINUX_CAPABILITY_U32S_3` as `2`: <https://man7.org/linux/man-pages/man2/capget.2.html>.

**Fix.** Write both version-3 entries, splitting each `CapSet` into low and high `u32` words, for example `datap[0] = bits as u32` and `datap[1] = (bits >> 32) as u32` for `effective`, `permitted`, and `inheritable`.

### `kernel/src/syscall/madvise.rs` line 40

> ```diff
>     39            // perform a read at first
>     40            let mut buffer = vec![0u8; len];
>     41            ctx.user_space()
>     42                .read_bytes(start, &mut VmWriter::from(buffer.as_mut_slice()))?;
> ```

Excessive allocation (critical): `vec![0u8; len]` allocates one kernel byte per user byte before any range walk, so `madvise(addr, 1 << 40, MADV_NORMAL)` can attempt a terabyte allocation and OOM or panic the kernel. Linux `madvise()` rounds the length and validates/advises the mapped range; it should not need a kernel buffer as large as the user range: <https://man7.org/linux/man-pages/man2/madvise.2.html>.

**Fix.** Remove the full-range `Vec` allocation. Validate the range through VM metadata, or if pages must be faulted for a specific advice, walk bounded page-sized chunks without allocating `len` bytes.

### `kernel/src/syscall/madvise.rs` line 42

> ```diff
>     39            // perform a read at first
>     40            let mut buffer = vec![0u8; len];
>     41            ctx.user_space()
>     42                .read_bytes(start, &mut VmWriter::from(buffer.as_mut_slice()))?;
> ```

Incorrect syscall result (major): `read_bytes()` makes `MADV_NORMAL` and `MADV_SEQUENTIAL` depend on user read permission and actual byte copying. A mapped `PROT_NONE` range, for example, should not fail these conventional advice calls merely because it cannot be read; Linux documents these advice values as hints that do not change application semantics, except for `MADV_DONTNEED`: <https://man7.org/linux/man-pages/man2/madvise.2.html>.

**Fix.** Do not probe conventional advice by copying user bytes. Check that `start..end` is mapped using VM metadata and apply only the advisory state changes or return the documented syscall error.

### `kernel/src/syscall/madvise.rs` line 45

> ```diff
>     44        MadviseBehavior::MADV_DONTNEED => {
>     45            warn!("MADV_DONTNEED isn't implemented, do nothing for now.");
>     46        }
> ```

Incorrect syscall result (major): `MADV_DONTNEED` returns success while leaving the pages unchanged. For an anonymous private mapping containing nonzero data, Linux requires later accesses after successful `MADV_DONTNEED` to succeed with zero-fill-on-demand pages, so this reports success without doing the requested operation: <https://man7.org/linux/man-pages/man2/madvise.2.html>.

**Fix.** Implement `MADV_DONTNEED` by discarding resident pages while preserving the mapping, or do not advertise it as supported. Returning `0` with no effect is the wrong visible behavior.

### `kernel/src/syscall/madvise.rs` line 48

> ```diff
>     44        MadviseBehavior::MADV_DONTNEED => {
>     45            warn!("MADV_DONTNEED isn't implemented, do nothing for now.");
>     46        }
>     47        MadviseBehavior::MADV_FREE => madv_free(start, end, ctx)?,
>     48        _ => todo!(),
> ```

Reachable panic (critical): Passing a valid but unhandled advice such as `MADV_RANDOM` (`1`) reaches `_ => todo!()`, so an unprivileged `madvise()` call can panic the kernel instead of returning a syscall error or applying the documented advice. `MADV_RANDOM` is listed by Linux `madvise(2)`: <https://man7.org/linux/man-pages/man2/madvise.2.html>.

**Fix.** Handle every `MadviseBehavior` value explicitly. At minimum, include `MADV_RANDOM` with the conventional no-op/read-ahead hints and return `EINVAL` or another deliberate syscall error for advice values this kernel does not implement; do not leave `todo!()` reachable from user input.

### `kernel/src/syscall/madvise.rs` line 57

> ```diff
>     53 fn madv_free(start: Vaddr, end: Vaddr, ctx: &Context) -> Result<()> {
>     54     let user_space = ctx.user_space();
>     55     let root_vmar = user_space.root_vmar();
>     56     let advised_range = start..end;
>     57     let _ = root_vmar.remove_mapping(advised_range);
> ```

Incorrect syscall effect (major): `MADV_FREE` calls `remove_mapping()`, which removes the VMA for `start..end`; the next load or store in that range can fault as unmapped. Linux `MADV_FREE` keeps the mapping valid, lets later writes cancel the lazy free, and only makes reads see zero-fill after pages are actually freed: <https://man7.org/linux/man-pages/man2/madvise.2.html>.

**Fix.** Do not unmap the advised range for `MADV_FREE`. Track lazy-free state on eligible private anonymous pages and preserve the VMA, or return `EINVAL` for unsupported/non-eligible mappings until that behavior exists.

### `kernel/src/syscall/open.rs` line 28

> ```diff
>     27    let file_handle = {
>     28        let path = path.to_string_lossy();
>     29        let fs_path = FsPath::new(dirfd, path.as_ref())?;
> ```

Incorrect pathname handling (major): `to_string_lossy()` rewrites invalid UTF-8 pathname bytes before lookup. Linux pathnames are C strings of non-null bytes, so `openat(AT_FDCWD, b"\xff\0", O_CREAT, ...)` should operate on the byte name `0xff`, not silently create or open a different name containing `U+FFFD`: <https://man7.org/linux/man-pages/man7/pathname.7.html>.

**Fix.** Preserve pathname bytes through `FsPath` and lookup, or reject unsupported encodings with a deterministic error. Do not use lossy conversion for syscall pathnames.

## Security

### `kernel/src/syscall/madvise.rs` line 40

> ```diff
>     35	    match behavior {
>     36	        MadviseBehavior::MADV_NORMAL
>     37	        | MadviseBehavior::MADV_SEQUENTIAL
>     38	        | MadviseBehavior::MADV_WILLNEED => {
>     39	            // perform a read at first
>     40	            let mut buffer = vec![0u8; len];
>     41	            ctx.user_space()
>     42	                .read_bytes(start, &mut VmWriter::from(buffer.as_mut_slice()))?;
>     43	        }
> ```

`validate-at-boundaries` (major): `len` is user supplied and is accepted up to `isize::MAX`, then `sys_madvise` allocates `vec![0u8; len]` before any bounded validation. A hostile process can call `madvise(page_aligned_addr, very_large_len, MADV_NORMAL)` and force an unbounded kernel heap allocation; [`madvise(2)`](https://www.man7.org/linux/man-pages/man2/madvise.2.html) describes these advice values as performance hints, not as a contract requiring the kernel to copy the whole user range into kernel memory.

**Fix.** Do not allocate a buffer proportional to `len`. For `MADV_NORMAL` and `MADV_SEQUENTIAL`, accept the validated range and return success or store bounded VM advice metadata; for `MADV_WILLNEED`, fault or prefetch incrementally with a small bounded buffer or page walk.

### `kernel/src/syscall/madvise.rs` line 48

> ```diff
>     44	        MadviseBehavior::MADV_DONTNEED => {
>     45	            warn!("MADV_DONTNEED isn't implemented, do nothing for now.");
>     46	        }
>     47	        MadviseBehavior::MADV_FREE => madv_free(start, end, ctx)?,
>     48	        _ => todo!(),
>     49	    }
> ```

/bin/bash -lc "sed -n '261,520p' /tmp/tmp.FpPaTXVq1Z/wt1.on.md" in /tmp/tmp.FpPaTXVq1Z/wt1
 succeeded in 0ms:

`validate-at-boundaries` (major): `MadviseBehavior::try_from` accepts documented user-controlled advice values such as `MADV_RANDOM`, but the fallback arm is `todo!()`. A hostile process can pass `behavior = 1` and panic the kernel from the syscall boundary instead of getting a normal syscall result; [`madvise(2)`](https://www.man7.org/linux/man-pages/man2/madvise.2.html) lists `MADV_RANDOM` as a conventional advice value.

**Fix.** Handle every accepted `MadviseBehavior` without panicking. Implement the supported values, ignore no-op advice where appropriate, or return an explicit error such as `EINVAL`/`ENOSYS` for unsupported values before reaching internal code.

## Documentation

### `kernel/src/syscall/capget.rs` line 20

> ```diff
> kernel/src/syscall/capget.rs:
> 20:     if cap_user_header.version != LINUX_CAPABILITY_VERSION_3 {
> 29:     if header_pid != 0 && header_pid != ctx.process.pid() {
> 41:         // Note we silently drop the upper capabilities here.
> 43:         effective: effective_capset.as_u32(),
> 
> book/src/kernel/linux-compatibility/README.md:
> 148: | 125     | capget                 | supported      |     |
> ```

`linux-compat-docs` (major): `sys_capget` exposes several user-visible compatibility limits: `cap_user_header_t.version` must be `LINUX_CAPABILITY_VERSION_3`, `cap_user_header_t.pid` must be `0` or `ctx.process.pid()`, and `cap_user_data_t` output drops the upper capability bits. `book/src/kernel/linux-compatibility/README.md` still marks `capget` as supported with no limitation and there is no `capget` subsection in `book/src/kernel/linux-compatibility/limitations-on-system-calls/namespaces-cgroups-and-security.md`. The Linux [`capget(2)` manual](https://man7.org/linux/man-pages/man2/capget.2.html) documents version probing, `datap[0]`/`datap[1]` for 64-bit capabilities, and querying any process via `hdrp->pid`, so these limitations need to be documented.

**Fix.** Add a `capget` Linux Compatibility subsection and link it from the syscall table. Document the supported `LINUX_CAPABILITY_VERSION_3` subset, current-process-only `pid` handling, and lower-32-bit capability reporting, and update the matching coverage artifact accordingly.

### `kernel/src/syscall/madvise.rs` line 47

`linux-compat-docs` (major): `sys_madvise` now handles `MadviseBehavior::MADV_FREE`, but `book/src/kernel/linux-compatibility/limitations-on-system-calls/memory-management.md` still lists `MADV_FREE` as unsupported. The Linux [`madvise(2)` manual](https://man7.org/linux/man-pages/man2/madvise.2.html) defines `MADV_FREE` as a real advice value, so the compatibility docs will tell users this syscall behavior is unavailable even though the implementation accepts it.

**Fix.** Update the `madvise` Linux Compatibility entry and the matching `.scml` coverage artifact to move `MADV_FREE` out of the unsupported list and describe the current `MADV_FREE` semantics or limitations.

 # Summary
 
-<!-- SUMMARY -->
+The reviewed syscall code keeps the entry points small and generally follows the existing syscall-handler shape, but `sys_madvise` has the highest-risk issues: user-controlled advice can reach a kernel `todo!()`, and conventional advice paths allocate a kernel buffer proportional to the supplied range. Several `madvise` behaviors also report success while doing something observably different from Linux, especially `MADV_DONTNEED` and `MADV_FREE`.

Structurally, the main cleanup is to make the externally sourced constants and limitations easier to maintain by citing authoritative sources, narrowing visibility, and updating the Linux-compatibility docs to match the implementation.

 Reachable panic (critical): Passing a valid but unhandled advice such as `MADV_RANDOM` (`1`) reaches `_ => todo!()`, so an unprivileged `madvise()` call can panic the kernel instead of returning a syscall error or applying the documented advice. `MADV_RANDOM` is listed by Linux `madvise(2)`: <https://man7.org/linux/man-pages/man2/madvise.2.html>.
 
-**Fix.** Handle every `MadviseBehavior` value explicitly. At minimum, include `MADV_RANDOM` with the conventional no-op/read-ahead hints and return `EINVAL` or another deliberate syscall error for advice values this kernel does not implement; do not leave `todo!()` reachable from user input.
+**Fix.** Shared with the security finding at `kernel/src/syscall/madvise.rs` line 48: handle every `MadviseBehavior` value explicitly. At minimum, include `MADV_RANDOM` with the conventional no-op/read-ahead hints and return `EINVAL` or another deliberate syscall error for advice values this kernel does not implement; do not leave `todo!()` reachable from user input.
 
 ### `kernel/src/syscall/madvise.rs` line 57
 
@@ -246,7 +248,7 @@
 
 `validate-at-boundaries` (major): `len` is user supplied and is accepted up to `isize::MAX`, then `sys_madvise` allocates `vec![0u8; len]` before any bounded validation. A hostile process can call `madvise(page_aligned_addr, very_large_len, MADV_NORMAL)` and force an unbounded kernel heap allocation; [`madvise(2)`](https://www.man7.org/linux/man-pages/man2/madvise.2.html) describes these advice values as performance hints, not as a contract requiring the kernel to copy the whole user range into kernel memory.
 
-**Fix.** Do not allocate a buffer proportional to `len`. For `MADV_NORMAL` and `MADV_SEQUENTIAL`, accept the validated range and return success or store bounded VM advice metadata; for `MADV_WILLNEED`, fault or prefetch incrementally with a small bounded buffer or page walk.
+**Fix.** Shared with the correctness finding at `kernel/src/syscall/madvise.rs` line 40: do not allocate a buffer proportional to `len`. For `MADV_NORMAL` and `MADV_SEQUENTIAL`, accept the validated range and return success or store bounded VM advice metadata; for `MADV_WILLNEED`, fault or prefetch incrementally with a bounded page walk.
 
 ### `kernel/src/syscall/madvise.rs` line 48
 
@@ -261,7 +263,7 @@
 
 `validate-at-boundaries` (major): `MadviseBehavior::try_from` accepts documented user-controlled advice values such as `MADV_RANDOM`, but the fallback arm is `todo!()`. A hostile process can pass `behavior = 1` and panic the kernel from the syscall boundary instead of getting a normal syscall result; [`madvise(2)`](https://www.man7.org/linux/man-pages/man2/madvise.2.html) lists `MADV_RANDOM` as a conventional advice value.
 
-**Fix.** Handle every accepted `MadviseBehavior` without panicking. Implement the supported values, ignore no-op advice where appropriate, or return an explicit error such as `EINVAL`/`ENOSYS` for unsupported values before reaching internal code.
+**Fix.** Shared with the correctness finding at `kernel/src/syscall/madvise.rs` line 48: handle every accepted `MadviseBehavior` without panicking. Implement the supported values, ignore no-op advice where appropriate, or return an explicit error such as `EINVAL`/`ENOSYS` for unsupported values before reaching internal code.

 `linux-compat-docs` (major): `sys_capget` exposes several user-visible compatibility limits: `cap_user_header_t.version` must be `LINUX_CAPABILITY_VERSION_3`, `cap_user_header_t.pid` must be `0` or `ctx.process.pid()`, and `cap_user_data_t` output drops the upper capability bits. `book/src/kernel/linux-compatibility/README.md` still marks `capget` as supported with no limitation and there is no `capget` subsection in `book/src/kernel/linux-compatibility/limitations-on-system-calls/namespaces-cgroups-and-security.md`. The Linux [`capget(2)` manual](https://man7.org/linux/man-pages/man2/capget.2.html) documents version probing, `datap[0]`/`datap[1]` for 64-bit capabilities, and querying any process via `hdrp->pid`, so these limitations need to be documented.
 
-**Fix.** Add a `capget` Linux Compatibility subsection and link it from the syscall table. Document the supported `LINUX_CAPABILITY_VERSION_3` subset, current-process-only `pid` handling, and lower-32-bit capability reporting, and update the matching coverage artifact accordingly.
+**Fix.** Add a `capget` Linux Compatibility subsection and link it from the syscall table. Document the supported `LINUX_CAPABILITY_VERSION_3` subset, current-process-only `pid` handling, and lower-32-bit capability reporting.

 `linux-compat-docs` (major): `sys_madvise` now handles `MadviseBehavior::MADV_FREE`, but `book/src/kernel/linux-compatibility/limitations-on-system-calls/memory-management.md` still lists `MADV_FREE` as unsupported. The Linux [`madvise(2)` manual](https://man7.org/linux/man-pages/man2/madvise.2.html) defines `MADV_FREE` as a real advice value, so the compatibility docs will tell users this syscall behavior is unavailable even though the implementation accepts it.

 # Summary
 
-<!-- SUMMARY -->
+The reviewed syscall code keeps the entry points small and generally follows the existing syscall-handler shape, but `sys_madvise` has the highest-risk issues: user-controlled advice can reach a kernel `todo!()`, and conventional advice paths allocate a kernel buffer proportional to the supplied range. Several `madvise` behaviors also report success while doing something observably different from Linux, especially `MADV_DONTNEED` and `MADV_FREE`.
 
+The next largest compatibility gap is `sys_capget`: version probing, target-`pid` lookup, and version-3 upper capability words do not match the Linux ABI. `sys_openat` also rewrites non-UTF-8 pathname bytes through `to_string_lossy()`, which can operate on a different filename than the caller supplied. Structurally, the main cleanup is to make the externally sourced constants and limitations easier to maintain by citing authoritative sources, narrowing visibility, and updating the Linux-compatibility docs to match the implementation.
+
 ## Maintainability
 
 ### `kernel/src/syscall/capget.rs` line 28
@@ -161,7 +163,7 @@
 
 Excessive allocation (critical): `vec![0u8; len]` allocates one kernel byte per user byte before any range walk, so `madvise(addr, 1 << 40, MADV_NORMAL)` can attempt a terabyte allocation and OOM or panic the kernel. Linux `madvise()` rounds the length and validates/advises the mapped range; it should not need a kernel buffer as large as the user range: <https://man7.org/linux/man-pages/man2/madvise.2.html>.
 
-**Fix.** Remove the full-range `Vec` allocation. Validate the range through VM metadata, or if pages must be faulted for a specific advice, walk bounded page-sized chunks without allocating `len` bytes.
+**Fix.** Shared with the security finding at `kernel/src/syscall/madvise.rs` line 40: remove the full-range `Vec` allocation. Validate the range through VM metadata, or if pages must be faulted for a specific advice, walk bounded page-sized chunks without allocating `len` bytes.

# Summary

The reviewed syscall code keeps the entry points small and generally follows the existing syscall-handler shape, but `sys_madvise` has the highest-risk issues: user-controlled advice can reach a kernel `todo!()`, and conventional advice paths allocate a kernel buffer proportional to the supplied range. Several `madvise` behaviors also report success while doing something observably different from Linux, especially `MADV_DONTNEED` and `MADV_FREE`.

The next largest compatibility gap is `sys_capget`: version probing, target-`pid` lookup, and version-3 upper capability words do not match the Linux ABI. `sys_openat` also rewrites non-UTF-8 pathname bytes through `to_string_lossy()`, which can operate on a different filename than the caller supplied. Structurally, the main cleanup is to make the externally sourced constants and limitations easier to maintain by citing authoritative sources, narrowing visibility, and updating the Linux-compatibility docs to match the implementation.

 ### `kernel/src/syscall/capget.rs` line 28

 Excessive allocation (critical): `vec![0u8; len]` allocates one kernel byte per user byte before any range walk, so `madvise(addr, 1 << 40, MADV_NORMAL)` can attempt a terabyte allocation and OOM or panic the kernel. Linux `madvise()` rounds the length and validates/advises the mapped range; it should not need a kernel buffer as large as the user range: <https://man7.org/linux/man-pages/man2/madvise.2.html>.
 
-**Fix.** Remove the full-range `Vec` allocation. Validate the range through VM metadata, or if pages must be faulted for a specific advice, walk bounded page-sized chunks without allocating `len` bytes.

# Expected defects

1. location: kernel/src/syscall/madvise.rs lines 35-50 (persona: development, grounding: unsupported MADV behavior must not panic, severity: major)
   defect: `MadviseBehavior` defines `MADV_NOHUGEPAGE`, but the match in `sys_madvise` has no arm for it. The value therefore reaches the catch-all `_ => todo!()` branch. A Podman call using `madvise(..., MADV_NOHUGEPAGE)` causes a kernel panic even though this Asterinas build does not support huge pages and can safely treat the advice as a no-op.
   MATCH IF: A reviewer should flag the reachable `todo!()` panic for the declared `MADV_NOHUGEPAGE` value and require a non-panicking unsupported-operation behavior, such as warning and returning success.

2. location: kernel/src/syscall/capget.rs lines 20-49 (persona: development, grounding: match capget version negotiation and null-data semantics, severity: major)
   defect: `sys_capget` immediately returns `EINVAL` when the requested capability version is unsupported, without writing `LINUX_CAPABILITY_VERSION_3` back through the header pointer. It also unconditionally writes the capability result to `cap_user_data_addr`, so a null data pointer cannot return success. The Linux capget contract uses an unsupported-version call to report the supported version, and permits a null data pointer for the version-query case. The base implementation therefore breaks Podman's capability probing protocol.
   MATCH IF: A reviewer should flag both missing parts of the version-query contract: the supported version must be written back on an unsupported request, and a null data pointer must not be dereferenced or rejected when the call is only querying the ABI version.

3. location: kernel/src/syscall/open.rs lines 21-40 (persona: development, grounding: reject empty pathname for openat, severity: major)
   defect: `sys_openat` reads the pathname and passes it directly to `FsPath::new` and the resolver. With an empty pathname, it does not return the required `ENOENT` error at the syscall boundary and instead relies on lower-level path handling, which does not provide the Linux `openat` behavior expected by Podman. `openat` has no `AT_EMPTY_PATH` option that would make an empty pathname valid.
   MATCH IF: A reviewer should flag that an empty pathname reaches generic path resolution and require `sys_openat` to return `ENOENT` explicitly before resolution.

# Summary

The reviewed syscall code keeps the entry points small and generally follows the existing syscall-handler shape, but `sys_madvise` has the highest-risk issues: user-controlled advice can reach a kernel `todo!()`, and conventional advice paths allocate a kernel buffer proportional to the supplied range. Several `madvise` behaviors also report success while doing something observably different from Linux, especially `MADV_DONTNEED` and `MADV_FREE`.

The next largest compatibility gap is `sys_capget`: version probing, target-`pid` lookup, and version-3 upper capability words do not match the Linux ABI. `sys_openat` also rewrites non-UTF-8 pathname bytes through `to_string_lossy()`, which can operate on a different filename than the caller supplied. Structurally, the main cleanup is to make the externally sourced constants and limitations easier to maintain by citing authoritative sources, narrowing visibility, and updating the Linux-compatibility docs to match the implementation.

## Maintainability

### `kernel/src/syscall/capget.rs` line 28

> ```diff
>     // Capget only query current process's credential. Namely, it only allows header->pid == 0
>     // or header->pid == getpid(), which are equivalent.
>     // See https://linux.die.net/man/2/capget (Section. With VFS capability support) for details.
> ```

`cite-sources` (minor): The `capget` comment cites `linux.die.net` and the `With VFS capability support` section, but the authoritative `capget(2)` page at <https://man7.org/linux/man-pages/man2/capget.2.html> uses that section for `capset()` restrictions; this sends readers to the wrong contract for the local `pid` limitation.

**Fix.** Cite the authoritative `man7.org` page and phrase the comment as an Asterinas implementation limitation, or update the comment to match the actual `capget(2)` contract.

### `kernel/src/syscall/madvise.rs` line 24

> ```diff
>     if len > isize::MAX as usize {
>         return_errno_with_message!(Errno::EINVAL, "len align overflow");
>     }
> ```

`error-message-format` (nit): The error message `len align overflow` is terse and grammatically unclear; it does not tell the next reader or caller which condition made `len` invalid.

**Fix.** Use a specific Linux-style message such as `` `len` is too large to align to a page boundary ``.

### `kernel/src/syscall/madvise.rs` line 39

> ```diff
>         MadviseBehavior::MADV_NORMAL
>         | MadviseBehavior::MADV_SEQUENTIAL
>         | MadviseBehavior::MADV_WILLNEED => {
>             // perform a read at first
>             let mut buffer = vec![0u8; len];
> ```

`explain-why` (minor): The comment `perform a read at first` only paraphrases the following `read_bytes` call and does not explain why `MADV_NORMAL`, `MADV_SEQUENTIAL`, and `MADV_WILLNEED` are implemented by reading the whole advised range.

**Fix.** Remove the comment if the code is self-explanatory, or replace it with the design reason for this approximation, for example that these advice modes are handled by prefaulting readable pages.

### `kernel/src/syscall/madvise.rs` line 63

> ```diff
> #[repr(i32)]
> #[derive(Debug, Clone, Copy, TryFromInt)]
> #[expect(non_camel_case_types)]
> /// This definition is the same from linux
> ```

`alphabetical-attrs` (nit): `#[derive(...)]` is placed before `#[expect(non_camel_case_types)]`, and the derived traits are not alphabetically sorted (`Debug` precedes `Clone` and `Copy`).

**Fix.** Put non-derive attributes first in alphabetical order and keep `#[derive(...)]` last with sorted traits:

```rust
#[expect(non_camel_case_types)]
#[repr(i32)]
#[derive(Clone, Copy, Debug, TryFromInt)]
```

### `kernel/src/syscall/madvise.rs` line 65

> ```diff
> #[expect(non_camel_case_types)]
> /// This definition is the same from linux
> pub enum MadviseBehavior {
> ```

`cite-sources` (minor): The doc comment says `MadviseBehavior` is `the same from linux`, but it does not cite the Linux UAPI or `madvise(2)` source for these externally defined numeric values, so future updates require archaeology.

**Fix.** Replace it with a source-backed doc comment, for example:

```rust
/// Linux `madvise(2)` advice values.
///
/// Values follow Linux `include/uapi/asm-generic/mman-common.h`; behavior is described in
/// <https://man7.org/linux/man-pages/man2/madvise.2.html>.
```

### `kernel/src/syscall/madvise.rs` line 66

> ```diff
> /// This definition is the same from linux
> pub enum MadviseBehavior {
>     MADV_NORMAL = 0,
> ```

`narrow-visibility` (minor): `MadviseBehavior` is declared `pub`, but the reviewed file is its only consumer; this exposes an internal syscall parsing detail without an actual external user.

**Fix.** Make `MadviseBehavior` private until another module needs it: change `pub enum MadviseBehavior` to `enum MadviseBehavior`.

## Correctness

### `kernel/src/syscall/capget.rs` line 20

> ```diff
>     17    let cap_user_header: cap_user_header_t =
>     18        user_space.read_val::<cap_user_header_t>(cap_user_header_addr)?;
>     19
>     20    if cap_user_header.version != LINUX_CAPABILITY_VERSION_3 {
>     21        return_errno_with_message!(Errno::EINVAL, "not supported (capability version is not 3)");
>     22    };
> ```

Incorrect syscall result (major): When `hdrp->version` is unsupported, Linux `capget()` must fail with `EINVAL` and write the kernel's preferred capability version back to `hdrp->version`; this returns `EINVAL` without updating the user header, so version-probing callers cannot discover `LINUX_CAPABILITY_VERSION_3`: <https://man7.org/linux/man-pages/man2/capget.2.html>.

**Fix.** Make `cap_user_header` mutable, set `cap_user_header.version = LINUX_CAPABILITY_VERSION_3`, write it back to `cap_user_header_addr`, and then return `EINVAL` on unsupported versions.

### `kernel/src/syscall/capget.rs` line 29

> ```diff
>     24    // Extract target pid and validate whether it represents the current process.
>     25    let header_pid = cap_user_header.pid;
>     26    // Capget only query current process's credential. Namely, it only allows header->pid == 0
>     27    // or header->pid == getpid(), which are equivalent.
>     28    // See https://linux.die.net/man/2/capget (Section. With VFS capability support) for details.
>     29    if header_pid != 0 {prompt}{prompt} header_pid != ctx.process.pid() {
> ```

Incorrect syscall result (major): `capget()` rejects any `hdrp->pid` other than `0` or the current process, but Linux allows `capget()` to probe another process/thread by `pid`; the VFS-only-current restriction applies to `capset()`, not `capget()`: <https://man7.org/linux/man-pages/man2/capget.2.html>.

**Fix.** For `capget()`, look up the target thread/process when `header_pid != 0`, return `ESRCH` if it does not exist, and report that target's credentials. Keep the current-process restriction only in `capset()`.

### `kernel/src/syscall/capget.rs` line 43

> ```diff
>     40    let result = cap_user_data_t {
>     41        // Note we silently drop the upper capabilities here.
>     42        // This behavior is considered fail-safe behavior.
>     43        effective: effective_capset.as_u32(),
>     44        permitted: permitted_capset.as_u32(),
>     45        inheritable: inheritable_capset.as_u32(),
>     46    };
>     47
>     48    user_space.write_val(cap_user_data_addr, {prompt}result)?;
> ```

Incorrect syscall result (major): `LINUX_CAPABILITY_VERSION_3` uses two `cap_user_data_t` entries, but this writes only one and truncates every capability above bit `31`. A process with `CAP_BPF` or `CAP_CHECKPOINT_RESTORE` set will get `0` in the upper capability word instead of the real value. Linux documents `_LINUX_CAPABILITY_U32S_3` as `2`: <https://man7.org/linux/man-pages/man2/capget.2.html>.

**Fix.** Write both version-3 entries, splitting each `CapSet` into low and high `u32` words, for example `datap[0] = bits as u32` and `datap[1] = (bits >> 32) as u32` for `effective`, `permitted`, and `inheritable`.

### `kernel/src/syscall/madvise.rs` line 40

> ```diff
>     39            // perform a read at first
>     40            let mut buffer = vec![0u8; len];
>     41            ctx.user_space()
>     42                .read_bytes(start, {prompt}mut VmWriter::from(buffer.as_mut_slice()))?;
> ```

Excessive allocation (critical): `vec![0u8; len]` allocates one kernel byte per user byte before any range walk, so `madvise(addr, 1 << 40, MADV_NORMAL)` can attempt a terabyte allocation and OOM or panic the kernel. Linux `madvise()` rounds the length and validates/advises the mapped range; it should not need a kernel buffer as large as the user range: <https://man7.org/linux/man-pages/man2/madvise.2.html>.

**Fix.** Shared with the security finding at `kernel/src/syscall/madvise.rs` line 40: remove the full-range `Vec` allocation. Validate the range through VM metadata, or if pages must be faulted for a specific advice, walk bounded page-sized chunks without allocating `len` bytes.

### `kernel/src/syscall/madvise.rs` line 42

> ```diff
>     39            // perform a read at first
>     40            let mut buffer = vec![0u8; len];
>     41            ctx.user_space()
>     42                .read_bytes(start, {prompt}mut VmWriter::from(buffer.as_mut_slice()))?;
> ```

Incorrect syscall result (major): `read_bytes()` makes `MADV_NORMAL` and `MADV_SEQUENTIAL` depend on user read permission and actual byte copying. A mapped `PROT_NONE` range, for example, should not fail these conventional advice calls merely because it cannot be read; Linux documents these advice values as hints that do not change application semantics, except for `MADV_DONTNEED`: <https://man7.org/linux/man-pages/man2/madvise.2.html>.

**Fix.** Do not probe conventional advice by copying user bytes. Check that `start..end` is mapped using VM metadata and apply only the advisory state changes or return the documented syscall error.

### `kernel/src/syscall/madvise.rs` line 45

> ```diff
>     44        MadviseBehavior::MADV_DONTNEED => {
>     45            warn!("MADV_DONTNEED isn't implemented, do nothing for now.");
>     46        }
> ```

Incorrect syscall result (major): `MADV_DONTNEED` returns success while leaving the pages unchanged. For an anonymous private mapping containing nonzero data, Linux requires later accesses after successful `MADV_DONTNEED` to succeed with zero-fill-on-demand pages, so this reports success without doing the requested operation: <https://man7.org/linux/man-pages/man2/madvise.2.html>.

**Fix.** Implement `MADV_DONTNEED` by discarding resident pages while preserving the mapping, or do not advertise it as supported. Returning `0` with no effect is the wrong visible behavior.

### `kernel/src/syscall/madvise.rs` line 48

> ```diff
>     44        MadviseBehavior::MADV_DONTNEED => {
>     45            warn!("MADV_DONTNEED isn't implemented, do nothing for now.");
>     46        }
>     47        MadviseBehavior::MADV_FREE => madv_free(start, end, ctx)?,
>     48        _ => todo!(),
> ```

Reachable panic (critical): Passing a valid but unhandled advice such as `MADV_RANDOM` (`1`) reaches `_ => todo!()`, so an unprivileged `madvise()` call can panic the kernel instead of returning a syscall error or applying the documented advice. `MADV_RANDOM` is listed by Linux `madvise(2)`: <https://man7.org/linux/man-pages/man2/madvise.2.html>.

**Fix.** Shared with the security finding at `kernel/src/syscall/madvise.rs` line 48: handle every `MadviseBehavior` value explicitly. At minimum, include `MADV_RANDOM` with the conventional no-op/read-ahead hints and return `EINVAL` or another deliberate syscall error for advice values this kernel does not implement; do not leave `todo!()` reachable from user input.

### `kernel/src/syscall/madvise.rs` line 57

> ```diff
>     53 fn madv_free(start: Vaddr, end: Vaddr, ctx: {prompt}Context) -> Result<()> {
>     54     let user_space = ctx.user_space();
>     55     let root_vmar = user_space.root_vmar();
>     56     let advised_range = start..end;
>     57     let _ = root_vmar.remove_mapping(advised_range);
> ```

Incorrect syscall effect (major): `MADV_FREE` calls `remove_mapping()`, which removes the VMA for `start..end`; the next load or store in that range can fault as unmapped. Linux `MADV_FREE` keeps the mapping valid, lets later writes cancel the lazy free, and only makes reads see zero-fill after pages are actually freed: <https://man7.org/linux/man-pages/man2/madvise.2.html>.

**Fix.** Do not unmap the advised range for `MADV_FREE`. Track lazy-free state on eligible private anonymous pages and preserve the VMA, or return `EINVAL` for unsupported/non-eligible mappings until that behavior exists.

### `kernel/src/syscall/open.rs` line 28

> ```diff
>     27    let file_handle = {
>     28        let path = path.to_string_lossy();
>     29        let fs_path = FsPath::new(dirfd, path.as_ref())?;
> ```

Incorrect pathname handling (major): `to_string_lossy()` rewrites invalid UTF-8 pathname bytes before lookup. Linux pathnames are C strings of non-null bytes, so `openat(AT_FDCWD, b"\xff\0", O_CREAT, ...)` should operate on the byte name `0xff`, not silently create or open a different name containing `U+FFFD`: <https://man7.org/linux/man-pages/man7/pathname.7.html>.

**Fix.** Preserve pathname bytes through `FsPath` and lookup, or reject unsupported encodings with a deterministic error. Do not use lossy conversion for syscall pathnames.

## Security

### `kernel/src/syscall/madvise.rs` line 40

> ```diff
>     35	    match behavior {
>     36	        MadviseBehavior::MADV_NORMAL
>     37	        | MadviseBehavior::MADV_SEQUENTIAL
>     38	        | MadviseBehavior::MADV_WILLNEED => {
>     39	            // perform a read at first
>     40	            let mut buffer = vec![0u8; len];
>     41	            ctx.user_space()
>     42	                .read_bytes(start, {prompt}mut VmWriter::from(buffer.as_mut_slice()))?;
>     43	        }
> ```

`validate-at-boundaries` (major): `len` is user supplied and is accepted up to `isize::MAX`, then `sys_madvise` allocates `vec![0u8; len]` before any bounded validation. A hostile process can call `madvise(page_aligned_addr, very_large_len, MADV_NORMAL)` and force an unbounded kernel heap allocation; [`madvise(2)`](https://www.man7.org/linux/man-pages/man2/madvise.2.html) describes these advice values as performance hints, not as a contract requiring the kernel to copy the whole user range into kernel memory.

**Fix.** Shared with the correctness finding at `kernel/src/syscall/madvise.rs` line 40: do not allocate a buffer proportional to `len`. For `MADV_NORMAL` and `MADV_SEQUENTIAL`, accept the validated range and return success or store bounded VM advice metadata; for `MADV_WILLNEED`, fault or prefetch incrementally with a bounded page walk.

### `kernel/src/syscall/madvise.rs` line 48

> ```diff
>     44	        MadviseBehavior::MADV_DONTNEED => {
>     45	            warn!("MADV_DONTNEED isn't implemented, do nothing for now.");
>     46	        }
>     47	        MadviseBehavior::MADV_FREE => madv_free(start, end, ctx)?,
>     48	        _ => todo!(),
>     49	    }
> ```

`validate-at-boundaries` (major): `MadviseBehavior::try_from` accepts documented user-controlled advice values such as `MADV_RANDOM`, but the fallback arm is `todo!()`. A hostile process can pass `behavior = 1` and panic the kernel from the syscall boundary instead of getting a normal syscall result; [`madvise(2)`](https://www.man7.org/linux/man-pages/man2/madvise.2.html) lists `MADV_RANDOM` as a conventional advice value.

**Fix.** Shared with the correctness finding at `kernel/src/syscall/madvise.rs` line 48: handle every accepted `MadviseBehavior` without panicking. Implement the supported values, ignore no-op advice where appropriate, or return an explicit error such as `EINVAL`/`ENOSYS` for unsupported values before reaching internal code.

## Documentation

### `kernel/src/syscall/capget.rs` line 20

> ```diff
> kernel/src/syscall/capget.rs:
> 20:     if cap_user_header.version != LINUX_CAPABILITY_VERSION_3 {
> 29:     if header_pid != 0 {prompt}{prompt} header_pid != ctx.process.pid() {
> 41:         // Note we silently drop the upper capabilities here.
> 43:         effective: effective_capset.as_u32(),
> 
> book/src/kernel/linux-compatibility/README.md:
> 148: | 125     | capget                 | ✅             |     |
> ```

`linux-compat-docs` (major): `sys_capget` exposes several user-visible compatibility limits: `cap_user_header_t.version` must be `LINUX_CAPABILITY_VERSION_3`, `cap_user_header_t.pid` must be `0` or `ctx.process.pid()`, and `cap_user_data_t` output drops the upper capability bits. `book/src/kernel/linux-compatibility/README.md` still marks `capget` as supported with no limitation and there is no `capget` subsection in `book/src/kernel/linux-compatibility/limitations-on-system-calls/namespaces-cgroups-and-security.md`. The Linux [`capget(2)` manual](https://man7.org/linux/man-pages/man2/capget.2.html) documents version probing, `datap[0]`/`datap[1]` for 64-bit capabilities, and querying any process via `hdrp->pid`, so these limitations need to be documented.

**Fix.** Add a `capget` Linux Compatibility subsection and link it from the syscall table. Document the supported `LINUX_CAPABILITY_VERSION_3` subset, current-process-only `pid` handling, and lower-32-bit capability reporting.

`linux-compat-docs` (major): `sys_madvise` now handles `MadviseBehavior::MADV_FREE`, but `book/src/kernel/linux-compatibility/limitations-on-system-calls/memory-management.md` still lists `MADV_FREE` as unsupported. The Linux [`madvise(2)` manual](https://man7.org/linux/man-pages/man2/madvise.2.html) defines `MADV_FREE` as a real advice value, so the compatibility docs will tell users this syscall behavior is unavailable even though the implementation accepts it.

**Fix.** Update the `madvise` Linux Compatibility entry to move `MADV_FREE` out of the unsupported list and describe the current `MADV_FREE` semantics or limitations.
